ii. Initial binder content selection 
Once the appropriate aggregate blends have been selected the ne xt step is to 
evaluate trial aggregate and bitumen blends by compacted the sp ecimens and 
assessing the volumetric properties for each blend. For each bl end at least two 
set of specimens are compacted the Super pave Gyratory Compacto r 
(explained below). The trial bitumen content can be selected fr om experience 
ROAD DESIGN MANUAL         PART 3 – Materials and Pavement Design for New Roads 
________________________________________________________________________  
_______________________________________________________________ _________ 
6-54 
Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 
 or using the guidelines provided in AASHTO R35. 
iii. Super pave Gyratory Compactor (SGC) 
Another fundamental change brought by the Super pave procedure is the 
method of compacting the bituminous mixes in the laboratory. Th is is carried 
out using a gyratory compactor which treats the trial mixes in a manner more 
akin to the action of traffic than the Marshall hammer. It is a lso much quicker 
and uses a sample diameter of 150mm in diameter and can accommo date 
mixtures containing maximum aggregate size up to 50mm (37.5mm n ominal 
size). 
The specification of the gyratory compactor is important and th e basic 
requirements are as follows: 
• a constant pressure of 600kPa on the compacting ram 
• a constant rate of rotation of 30 gyrations per minute 
• the mould should be positioned at a compaction angle of 1.250 
iv. Compaction of volumetric specimens 
The specimens are compacted either N design or N max as required. A minimum of two 
specimens are compacted at the trial blend’s estimated bitumen content at 0.5 
percent of the estimated bitumen content and at 1.0 percent abo ve. Four binder 
contents are required for the accomplishment of a Super pave mi x design. 
After compaction of the samples the volumetric properties of ea ch specimen are 
determined. The results of the laboratory testing are plotted t o provide curves of 
bitumen content versus air voids (VIM), voids in the mineral ag gregate (VMA) and 
voids filled with bitumen (VFB). 
The design requirements are presented in Table 6-29and Table 6- 30.

67%, for <0.3M esa 
7. For 9.5mm nominal maximum aggregate size, the specified rang e in VFB shall be 
73 to 76%, for ≥3M esa 
8. If the aggregate gradation passes below the ‘restricted zone ’ the filler/binder 
ratio range may be increased from 0.6 – 1.2 to 0.8-1.6. 
6.4.10 Design binder content 
After the data has been plotted the bitumen binder content that  will provide 4 
percent air voids at N des should be picked. Then the values of voids in the mineral 
aggregate (VMA) and voids filled with bitumen (VFB) are determi ned from the 
curves drawn as explained in sub-section 6.4.9.5. These values will then be 
checked for against mixture specifications. The recommended vol umetric design 
requirements are presented in Table 6-25. If the tests indicate  that the mixture 
does not meet the design requirements then the recommendations given in 
AASHTO R35 should be followed.

Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 
 6.4.10.1 Performance Tests 
Ascertaining that the mix composition of a bituminous mixture i s correct and that the 
VIM value does not fall below 3% is a key element of the mix de sign process. However, 
the degree of aggregate interlock and friction between particle s plays also a vital role in 
preventing shear failure. Recent developments in the highway co nstruction industry are 
moving towards the end-performance and end-product criteria of materials. It is 
therefore becoming more and more desirable, particularly for th e design of hot 
bituminous mixtures to carry out performance tests. Such tests include: 
• Determination of mix stiffness moduli: Indirect Tensile Tests  ( I T T )  o f f e r  
reliable and appropriate test methods to determine mix stiffnes s modulus. 
• Creep stiffness modulus: can be defined as a function between  s t r e s s  
(applied load) and permanent strain (deformation). It is determ ined by 
dynamic tests on core samples. This test would enable to rank b ituminous 
mixes in terms of deformation under traffic. 
• Wheel tracking: it has been found that it correlates well wit h performance of 
hot bituminous mixtures on site and could predict their rutting  behaviour. It is 
recommended that specifications be developed taking into accoun t the local 
conditions and construction materials. 
• Shear tests. 
6.4.11 Gap-graded Asphalt 
Gap-graded asphalt has a certain range of particle sizes missin g from the total 
aggregate grading. It generally consists of aggregate of a fair ly uniform size blended 
with sand and filler. As there is very little stone-to-stone co ntact in the compacted mix, 
the stability is derived from the cohesion of the sand-filler-b itumen mortar. 
One would expect that gap-graded asphalt would not be very resi stant to deformation 
because of the deficiency in mechanical strength. Even in tempe rate climates on heavily 
trafficked roads it has a tendency to rut quickly and therefore  probably is suitable only 
for medium to light traffic (T3, T4 and T5); it is becoming sup erseded by thin surfacings. 
Its main virtues are its flexibility, its fatigue resistance an d impermeability, and its 
durability, due to the good distribution of the voids structure  and the rounded shape of 
most of the fine aggregate. 
The gap-graded mixes specified are designed for thin wearing co urses (25-50mm). 
These mixes are impermeable, because of the comparatively high bitumen content and 
the good distribution of the voids structure. The coarse aggreg ate is limited to 55% by 
weight. “Low stone content” mixes (less than 40%) are easy to w ork and compact and 
are very tolerant to mix variations, whereas “High stone conten t” mixes (more than 45%) 
become sensitive to changes in bitumen content. 
Gradings and other properties are listed in Table 6-31:

6.4.11.1 Mix design method 
• Prepare mixture of fine aggregate and filler to give 1:6 rati o by mass of filler 
to fine aggregate retained on 0.075mm sieve. Determine the opti mum 
bitumen content which gives m aximum Marshall Quotient. 
• By adding coarse aggregate and reducing bitumen content the M arshall 
Quotient may be increased to the specified value. 20% coarse ag gregate will 
increase quotient by factor of approx. 1.5, 30% by 1.9, 40% by 2.4 and 55% 
by 3.0. For an increase in coarse aggregate the revised bitumen  content is 
given by: 
NBC = OBC  (100 - S)/100+ 2.3( S)/100 
where: NBC = Nominal Bitumen Content 
OBC= Optimum Bitumen Content S = % of 
coarse aggregate added 
• Prepare mixes in accordance with BS 594 at bitumen content fr om (2) to 
check specified requirements.

to rutting is not very high, sand asphalt is suitable only for light and medium traffic, i.e. 
Classes T4 and T5. 
6.3 Other Materials 
6.4.13 Recycled Asphalt Pavement (RAP) 6.4.13.1 Current Practice in Kenya 
It can be economically viable, and environmentally desirable, t o reclaim worn-out 
asphalt and granular pavements, re-process and re-use them in t he rehabilitated road. 
Implementation of the technology, however, requires significant  investment in specialist 
skills and equipment, appropriate Governmental pressure to carr y it out and the 
formulation of suitable specifications and/or methodologies to control the process. 
Details of the latter are presented in Appendix H of ORN 19 200 2 and summarized here. 
Presently in Kenya, the technology is not often used. On the ma jor road projects of the 
Northern Corridor route, RAP is produced by milling the old roa d surface, and either 
mixed with fresh aggregate and cement to make ‘cement-improved subbase’ and/or 
mixed with additional crushed stone to make an ‘upper subgrade’ . It could be that the 
strength of these re-processed layers is manifestly greater tha n the normal requirement 
in order to ensure that the life of the road will be long. 
The stockpiling of RAP prior to re-processing is a critical par t of the procedure. It is 
important that the variability of the RAP is well controlled. S tockpiled RAP tends to 
agglomerate and a crust forms, depending on the hardness of the  bitumen and the 
ambient temperature. To offset this tendency, it has been found  that the larger the 
stockpile the better. RAP readily absorbs moisture and it is be st stored under roofing in 
an open-sided building. 
ROAD DESIGN MANUAL         PART 3 – Materials and Pavement Design for New Roads

 6.4.13.2 RAP in improved subgrade layers 
An improved subgrade would only be used in the reconstruction o f a road pavement 
where (in the unlikely event) the in situ subgrade CBR is <5%. The existing UK 
specification requires RAP to meet the grading specification in  Table 6-33. The capping 
layer can consist of 100% of RAP providing the bitumen content is <10%. The recycled 
material can be laid to a maximum thickness of 200mm providing the required density is 
obtained. This is 95% of BS Heavy, BS 1377, Part 4, 1990. Alter natively, the material 
can be laid by method specification.

6.4.13.3 RAP in Subbase Layers 
The quality of the aggregate in the RAP should at least meet th e requirements for this

the blended material should be acceptable providing the bitumen  is hard enough not to 
hinder compaction and enable the required moisture content, whi ch is between the 
optimum moisture content and -2% of optimum moisture content ac hieved with the BS 
Vibrating Hammer test, BS 1377, Part 4, 1990.to be met. This is  also subject to a 
Trafficking Trial, whereby the compacted RAP is laid on a prepa red trial area 
constructed t a specified standard and then trafficked with a l oaded truck until 1000 esa 
have been applied. The mean deformation in the wheel paths must  be <30mm for the 
material to be acceptable. 
6.4.14 Modified Bitumens 
Modified bitumens are not oft en used in Kenya; they have been i n existence for more 
than 15 years. It may be because of bad experience or cost, or a combination of 
reasons. The main problem is that the field is a very specializ ed one; investigations 
have to be carried to identify the modifier best suited to the bitumen of choice because 
the beneficial effects are not guaranteed. 
Unmodified bitumen has weaknesses: it becomes rapidly more ‘flu id’ at high 
temperatures, facilitating rutting in asphalt and embedment in surface dressing: and in 
the intense light of the tropics it rapidly ages and hardens, a ccelerating cracking. 
Numerous proprietary products are commercially available to ame liorate these 
characteristics. The products can be categorized as follows: 
ROAD DESIGN MANUAL         PART 3 – Materials and Pavement Design for New Roads 
________________________________________________________________________  
_______________________________________________________________ _________ 
6-60 
Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 
 6.4.14.1 Polymers 
It is possible to create additives which are claimed to enhance  the good properties of 
bitumen whilst suppressing the bad properties. Ethyl Vinyl Acet ate (EVA), Styrene 
Butadiene Styrene (SBS) and Styrene Butadiene Rubber (SBR) are three such 
examples. They all increase the viscosity, and visco-elastic st iffness, of bitumen at high 
temperatures and increase fatigue life. The consequences of usi ng polymers to 
suppress the development of rutting and premature bitumen aging  are obvious. 
6.4.14.2 Rubbers 
It is claimed that natural rubber can be added to bitumen to en hance its resilience. Much 
of the motivation for this has been the desire to recycle used vehicle tyres and the 
benefits are still uncertain. 
6.4.14.3 Chemical additives 
Elemental sulphur and manganese have both been used as additive s. Sulphur becomes 
a liquid at a temperature greater than ≈1200C and is added to improve workability at 
high temperature. Manganese is used to increase bitumen viscosi ty and stiffness but 
with the disadvantage that bitumen becomes more brittle. 
6.4.15 Block Paving 
6.4.15.1 General 
A concrete block surface relies on the development of the mecha nical interlock between 
blocks to provide shear resistance and disperse traffic induced  s t r e s s .  A  f i r m  e d g e  
support is required to prevent ravelling at the edge of the sur face. Supplementary 
drainage measures are necessary to prevent saturation of the un derlying roadbed 
through the ingress of water. Concrete block surfaces are not s uitable for sections 
where the speed of heavy vehicles exceeds 60 km/h. They are mos t suitable for heavy 
vehicle parking areas and pedestrian walkways. 
6.4.15.2 Quality of Blocks 
Blocks may be plain or interlocking. Interlocking blocks, avail able in a variety of shapes, 
provide enhanced mechanical interlock, improving strength and d urability characteristics 
and resisting the punching loads and horizontal shear loads cau sed by the manoeuvring 
of heavy goods vehicles. Interlocking concrete pavement blocks are resistant to oil 
spillages and may be used in a variety of heavily stressed loca tions including 
intersections, weigh bridges, customs and toll barriers, servic e station forecourts, 
container and bus terminals, ports, bulk cargo handling areas: examples are shown in 
Figure 6-6. 
ROAD DESIGN MANUAL         PART 3 – Materials and Pavement Design for New Roads 
________________________________________________________________________  
_______________________________________________________________ _________ 
6-61 
Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 
  
Figure 6-6: Typical patterns for concrete blocks  
In order to ensure durability, interlocking blocks in heavily s tressed locations must be of 
consistent high quality and dimensional accuracy implying mass production under 
factory conditions. For the surfacing of low volume roads, plai n concrete blocks of more 
variable quality and dimensional accuracy may be produced by la bour-based means 
and can still perform well over their service life. 
Concrete paving blocks are typically of nominal size 200×100×65 mm, although the 
thickness of the last dimension may vary. The concrete for the block manufacture 
should be sound, impermeable, of minimum strength 30MPa and mad e with a maximum 
water cement ratio of 0.5. To ensure both economy and quality i t is strongly 
recommended that a mix design exercise is carried out in order to attain the required 
parameters for strength, durability and workability. It may be assumed that a great many 
blocks will be required and ensuring that the least amount of c ement is consumed to 
consistently reach but not greatly exceed those requirements wi ll deliver value for 
money. Accurate batching procedures and compliance with effecti ve curing regimes are 
also critical in this respect. 
Stone aggregate for the production of concrete blocks should be  strong, sound and 
angular and may be obtained from crushed rock, hand-knapped sto ne or screened 
natural gravel. The grading of the aggregates should respect th e envelope presented in 
Table 6-37. If natural river or beach gravel is used as a sourc e it should be hand-
knapped to ensure that each particle has two or more broken fac es. Igneous rocks like 
granite, basalt or quartzite and well-bonded sandstone are all suitable but generally not 
sedimentary rocks.

If the soaked CBR of the roadbed material is ≥ 6% (at 95% of AA STO standard) a single

construct an imported subgrade layer with a soaked CBR of ≥ 15%  and lay a single 
concrete pavement block layer. 
6.4.15.4 Construction for parking areas and walkways 
After completion of the underlying pavement construction as ind icated above, the coarse 
sand bedding should be evenly spread in a loose, uncompacted la yer. The compacted 
thickness should be 40mm for heavy vehicle parking areas and 30 mm for walkways. 
The moisture content of the sand bedding should be between 80% and 105% of the 
Optimum Moisture Content for the material. If the material has been saturated by rain 
should be removed and dried and brought to the stated acceptabl e limits. The sand 
bedding should be then compacted with a vibrating plate compact or until the surface 
achieves uniform density and compaction. A loose sand layer abo ut 10mm thickness is 
then spread and screeded to create a loose surface on which the  paving blocks will be 
laid. 
The type of pattern in which the paving blocks will be laid mus t then be decided. The 
order of laying is such that an open working face is maintained . The first row of paving 
blocks should be aligned against the edge restraint, or against  a string line. Alignment of 
the blocks should be checked frequently and adjustments made wh ere necessary. 
Where the pattern involves continuous lines, deflections off-li ne should not exceed 
15mm in 3000mm. the bond pattern should be maintained at all ti mes. Joint width for 
sand infilling should range from 2mm to 5mm. the area to be lai d should be completed 
as far as possible with whole paving blocks. Formation of bound aries and changes of

 direction should be carried out with blocks trimmed to shape an d size for the purpose, 
using a purpose-made block splitter or saw. 
To work round any obstruction, the obstruction should be surrou nded with concrete 
using aggregate of maximum size 10mm and compression strength o f 40N/mm2, to 
form a regular shape and then the paving blocks trimmed to abut  the obstruction should 
be completed as the laying proceeds and before compaction begin s. 
After laying of the paving blocks is complete the surface cours e should be compacted 
using the vibrating plate compactor with a plate area of not le ss than 0.25mm2, 
transmitting an effective force of not less than 75kN/m2 of plate at a frequency of 
vibration ranging from 75Hz to 100Hz. Compaction should be carr ied out as soon as 
possible after the laying of the paving blocks but not within 1  metre of any surface being 
laid. No area of paving should be left uncompacted at the compl etion of a day’s work. 
After compaction of the surface course, dry sand or crushed roc k fines complying with 
the specifications below shall be spread on the finished surfac e and brushed into the 
joints. The surface should then be vibrated using the plate com pactor to ensure 
complete filling of the joints by the fines; where necessary ad ditional fines should be

6.4.15.5 Construction for roads 
If the paving blocks are required for the road surfacing, the r oadbed is shaped to a 
camber of 3-5% and compacted to refusal density at a moisture c ontent close to the 
optimum. Careful quality control in respect of the camber and r egularity of the finished 
surface is required as any defects or irregularities in the fin ished surface of the roadbed 
will be reflected in the finished concrete block surface. 
Blocks on end are placed in a trench, firmly bedded and laid to  the final road level to 
provide an edge restraint with a vertical face that extends bel ow the underside of the 
bedding layer. The trench is backfilled with moist, well compac ted excavated material to 
firmly anchor the edge blocks in position, any excess material being shaped and 
compacted to form a slight berm that will offer some temporary support prior to the 
construction of gravel shoulders. 
Supplementary drainage measures should be provided to prevent a ny ingress of water 
through the surface from becoming trapped behind the edge suppo rt where it would 
otherwise penetrate the roadbed causing it to soften and loose strength and the 
concrete block surface to deform and ultimately fail. 
ROAD DESIGN MANUAL         PART 3 – Materials and Pavement Design for New Roads

_______________________________________________________________ _________ 
6-64 
Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 
 Coarse sand is placed on the roadbed; spread with a screed to a  uniform thickness 
following the roadbed camber then lightly compacted to form a 3 0mm thick bedding 
layer. 
For single layer construction, blocks are laid on the flat or o n edge in a herringbone 
pattern such that the longest dimension of each block runs at a n angle of 45° to the road 
centre-line. Each block is firmly bedded in place and laid to t he final road level by 
tapping it with a heavy metal hammer and a hardwood cushioning block. 
Blocks should be evenly placed such that joints are of a unifor m width of 2 –5mm. Some 
factory produced concrete blocks have integral protrusions that  act as spacers. 
Following compaction with two passes of a vibrating plate compa ctor to reduce any 
surface irregularities, fine sand is brushed and vibro-compacte d with a further two 
passes of the vibrating plate compactor to fully occupy the joi nts. As construction 
proceeds compaction should not take place within 1m of any open  working face. 
Blocks in the foundation layer of a two layer surface are laid flat in a conventional 
interlocking bond with the longest dimension of each block runn ing perpendicular to the 
road centre line. Each block is firmly bedded in place and laid  to the final road level as 
below. Half blocks are used to fill the voids in the lower laye r at the edges. 
Following compaction with a vibrating plate compactor, fine san d is brushed and vibro-
compacted to fill the joints leaving a ≈10mm layer of excess fi nes evenly spread over the 
surface of the foundation layer. The second layer of blocks is laid on edge in a 
herringbone pattern such that the longest dimension of each blo ck runs at an angle of 
45° to the road centre-line. The blocks are then placed, laid, compacted and finished in 
the same manner as above. 
To ensure an acceptable level of roughness the maximum deviatio n of the laid surface 
under a 3m straight edge should be less than 10mm and the diffe rence in level between 
adjacent blocks should not exceed 2mm. The triangular gaps at t he edge of the surface 
are filled with damp grout, comprising 1 part cement to 4 of co arse sand that is rammed 
firmly into place. The surface of blocks abutting drainage chan nels should be laid 3 – 
6mm above the lip of the channel.

6.4.16 Geosynthetic materials 
Unbound materials have nil tensile strength and sometimes it is  necessary to provide 
this by means of geosynthetic reinforcement. A successful fabri cation can make a very 
significant improvement to the deformation resistance of an unb ound material. On other 
occasions geosynthetic material is used as a separator to avoid  contamination of 
pavement layers by soil material; and also sometimes as additio nal strengthening to an 
asphalt overlay or inlay. 
6.4.16.1 Reinforcement 
i. Subgrade 
The reinforcement membrane imparts strength by interacting with  the shear strength of 
the material being reinforced. The reinforcement must therefore  achieve interlock with 
the individual particles. Thus, for soils with particle sizes o f the order of µm 
(micrometers), a membrane of appropriate texture must be used; whilst for granular 
materials a geogrid is more suitable. 
Together with the stress reinforcement through particle-to-part icle interlock there is also 
a contribution due to the friction of the soil particles with t he membrane. The main 
contribution of the membrane, however, is to withstand the tens ile stresses that may be 
suffered by the soil. Obviously, the support is greatest in the  plane of the membrane and 
decreases away from it, however, as yet, there is no method of predicting the zone of 
influence of the membrane. 
ii. Pavement 
Geogrids are increasingly being used to enhance the life of an asphalt inlay or overlay 
by slowing down the rate of crack formation and by providing ad ditional shear resistance 
against rutting, especially in high stress sites. A secondary b enefit is that cracks are 
prevented from opening even after penetrating the full depth of  the pavement. Geogrids 
are manufactured from steel, glass fibre, plastic and also asph alt. They are also placed 

ROAD DESIGN MANUAL         PART 3 – Materials and Pavement Design for New Roads 
________________________________________________________________________  
_______________________________________________________________ _________ 
6-66 
Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 
 into a layer of sprayed bitumen, applied at an approximate rate  of 1litre/m2 and in this 
situation act as a sealant, preventing water from entering even  after cracks have formed. 
They also have a role as separators, preventing reflective crac king. 
The geosynthetic used must therefore be matched with the purpos e and the material 
being reinforced. It is the responsibility of the engineer to a ssess the need for and the 
effectiveness of the varied products available in the marketpla ce. 
iii. Separator 
Materials used for this purpose are called geotextiles, and are  classed either as ‘woven’ 
or ‘non-woven’, and can be manufactured of plastic, glass fibre  or plant-based material. 
The main property is that they should permit water to flow free ly yet not soil. It is 
important to take account of the rupture strength possibly bein g exceeded by large 
particles or construction traffic. 
6.4.17 Hand-Packed Stone 
Hand-packed stone was a construction method common in the past in Kenya and used 
to build the Nairobi-Nakuru highway and many city streets in Na irobi, using a convenient 
stone source (Njiri) close to the city. Specifications were inc luded in the Ministry of 
Works Design Manual of 1970. It is still used, most commonly fo r bus bays and parking 
lots. Large stones, usually of 15cm size, are placed directly o nto the natural ground. The 
gaps between the stones are then in filled with smaller stones,  then rock fines, 
sometimes vibrated in with a hand operated machine, until the f ines completely cover 
the stones and present a level surface. The integrity of the su rface then depends how 
well the large stones have been packed together on the natural ground, how well the 
fines have been vibrated in, and how strong the natural ground is to start with. 
6.4.17.1 Material quality 
The large pieces of broken stone form an interlocking load-bear ing matrix in conjunction 
with the smaller stone chips that must be tightly wedged to fir mly anchor the whole layer, 
or layers, in place. Thus, load transfer takes place at relativ ely few contact points and 
the crushing strength of the stone will influence the rate of i ts degradation under traffic 
loading. The stone should be tough and durable and obtained fro m breaking down 
unweathered hard rock, which should ‘ring’ when struck with a g eological hammer; a 
hollow, dull sound is indicative of degradation. Crystalline ig neous rocks such as granite 
and basalt are preferable. The use of rounded cobbles or sedime ntary rock with weakly 
bonded bedding planes should be avoided. The finished surface i s durable and fairly 
impermeable but, as the hand-packed stone forms the trafficked surface, the riding 
quality is only moderate. 
The rock from which the stones are prepared should comply with the following 
parameters: 
• ACV max 40% 
• LAA max 45% 
• SSS max 12% 
ROAD DESIGN MANUAL         PART 3 – Materials and Pavement Design for New Roads 
________________________________________________________________________  
_______________________________________________________________ _________ 
6-67 
Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 
 The stones should have a minimum of flat or elongate fragments and free of other soft, 
deleterious material and be of such a grading and size that the y pack firmly when laid by 
hand. The larger stones should have a maximum dimension slightl y greater than the 
thickness of the required compacted layer and be of a shape acc eptable to the 
Engineer. The smaller stones should have a reasonably uniform g rading and be of a 
nominal size suitable for filling the spaces between the larger  stones. The fines should 
derive from tough and durable crushed stone, free from an exces s of soft and 
weathered fragments and clay, silt and have a grading indicated  in Table 6-40:

0.150 10 to 30 
0.075 0 to 20 
Table 6-37: Grading of fi nes of hand-packed stone 
If sandy soil has to be used it should comply with the followin g requirements: 
• consist of sand size and have a smooth grading 
• the weight   passing the 0.075mm sieve shall be less than the  weight passing 
the 5mm sieve 
• the PI shall be less than 5 
The integrity of the hand-packed stone surface is highly depend ent on the provision of 
adequate edge support, such as kerbstones, without which the la yer would progressively 
ravel from the edge inwards under the passage of traffic. The h and-packed stone surfacing 
disperses the stresses caused by traffic loading and acts as a barrier to erosion and 
pulverisation of the roadbed that would otherwise result from t yre friction. 
6.4.17.2 Roadbed 
Where the soaked CBR of the roadbed material is ≥ 5% (95% AASHT O standard) the 
recommended thickness of the stone packed surface is 150mm. If the CBR value is 
even lower a thickness of 200mm is recommended. The roadbed is shaped to a camber 
of 3-5% and compacted to refusal density at a moisture content close to the optimum. 
6.4.17.3 Construction 
The roadbed is shaped to a camber of 3 to 5% and compacted to r efusal at a moisture 
content close to the optimum. On the prepared area the individu al stones are placed by 
hand with the greatest dimension vertical and the largest and f lattest end downward. 
The greater number of stones shall be slightly higher than the thickness of the material 
when laid. 
After the placement of the stones and fines in the specified ma nner, the material shall be 
compacted, preferably with a grid roller and a vibratory roller , until the layer is thoroughly 
locked.

Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 
 After setting out the finished road line and level, a 250mm wid e by 200mm deep trench 
is excavated to accommodate kerbstones along each edge of the r oad. The minimum 
dimensions of the kerbstones should be 400, 200 and 100mm. The smallest face of 
each kerbstone is dressed so that it is flat and approximately perpendicular to the 
longest axis. 
The kerbstones, placed in the trench with their longest axis ve rtical and smallest face 
uppermost, are firmly bedded and laid to the final road level. The trench is backfilled with 
moist, well compacted excavated material to firmly anchor the k erbstones in position. 
Supplementary drainage measures should be provided to prevent a ny ingress of water 
through the surface from becoming trapped behind the kerbstone edge support where it 
would otherwise penetrate the roadbed causing it to soften and loose strength, and the 
hand-packed stone surface to deform and ultimately fail. The sa lient features are 
illustrated in Figure 6-8 below.

ROAD DESIGN MANUAL         PART 3 – Materials and Pavement Design for New Roads  
 
 
7-1 
Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 7 PAVEMENT STRUCTURAL DESIGN 
7.1 DESIGN PRINCIPLES 
7.1.1 Thicknesses and Materials Characteristics 
No pavement structure can be designed independently of the char acteristics of 
the pavement materials. Indeed, every material has a different behaviour which is 
largely influenced by the characteristics of the other pavement  layers. 
7.1.2 Design Period 
As mentioned in Chapter 2, the design period is the span of tim e considered 
appropriate for the pavement to function without major rehabili tation or 
reconstruction. Therefore, it is a fundamental parameter in pav ement design 
and management schemes.  
 
Each of the pavement structures proposed has been designed to c arry a 
certain cumulative traffic. When the pavement has carried the e xpected traffic, it 
will need to be strengthened so that it can continue to carry t raffic for a further 
period.  In this respect, it is necessary that paved roads be c ontinuously 
monitored, so that strengthening can be planned and constructed  in time, that is 
to say before extensive deterioration has occurred. 
 
It is assumed that, during the design period, only ordinary mai ntenance will be 
carried out; this will comprise shoulder and drainage system ma intenance, 
erosion and vegetation control, localised patching and periodic  resealing. This 
maintenance is, however, essential, and its neglect will seriou sly affect the 
pavement performance. 
 
7.1.3 Stage Construction 
Major roads can be constructed in stages as traffic loading dem ands and 
finances permit. If staged construction can be shown to be econ omical, then 
this policy is still appropriate, but the first stage of constr uction should be 
compatible with subsequent improvements. For example, it is kno wn that the 
performance of asphalt surfacings is dependent on the stiffness  of the 
underlying layer. Therefore, if it is proposed to provide aspha lt surfacing at a 
later date, then the initial pavement should be of adequate sti ffness. 
Staged construction may also be employed where there are likely  to be 
changes in traffic patterns as a result of the introduction of new traffic links or 
situations where settlement may occur due to poor ground condit ions. 
Particular care should be taken with staged construction of pav ements using 
bound layers because, if fatigue cracking of this initial layer  occurs, 
considerable additional expense may be incurred in the second c onstruction 
stage to prevent reflection of this cracking, thereby significa ntly increasing 
whole-of-life costs. 
ROAD DESIGN MANUAL         PART 3 – Materials and Pavement Design for New Roads  
 
 
7-2 
Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 In all cases the pavement should not be allowed to deteriorate to such an 
extent that extensive reconstruction, rather than simply streng thening, is 
required in the second stage.  
Other factors which should be assessed when considering staged 
construction include: 
 the economic and social consequences of pavement distress in t he 
first stage resulting from delays in constructing the second st age, 
 the cost of sidetracking and other provision for traffic durin g the 
second stage construction this is particularly significant in mountainous or swampy terrain, 
 the effects of raising the pavement level at the second stage on 
kerbs, culverts, guardrails and other road components, 
 the relative permeabilities of the subgrade and paving materia ls and 
the effect on pavement drainage by omission of the second stage  
material, 
 difficulties associated with achieving a high standard of cons truction 
with some paving materials if construction under traffic is nec essary. 
 
In some areas it is common practice to defer the placement of t he wearing 
course on asphalt-surfaced roads. This practice can lead to a s ignificant 
reduction in pavement life, depending on the length of delay in  placing the 
wearing course. This delay should be allowed for in determining  the design 
thicknesses of layers. For example, for a heavily-trafficked as phalt road, an 
additional 5 to 10 mm of asphalt may be required to be added to  t h e  f i n a l  
thickness of the asphalt layers if the placement of the wearing  course is 
deferred for two years. Where bound layer(s) are present, the m echanistic 
design process allows the likely life of the pavement configura tion of each 
stage to be determined.  
If placement of the wearing course is deferred some modificatio ns to surface 
drainage may be required to ensure water does not accumulate on  the road 
surface. 
In addition, consideration needs to be given to ensuring that t he temporary 
surface has adequate skid resistance. 
 
        
 
ROAD DESIGN MANUAL         PART 3 – Materials and Pavement Design for New Roads  
 
 
7-3 
Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 7.2 MECHANISTIC PAVEMENT DESIGN  
7.2.1 Introduction 
The mechanisms of deterioration that form the basis of the pave ment design 
are: 
 rutting in the subgrade,  
 fatigue cracking at the bottom of the bituminous layers and, 
 horizontal cracking at the bottom of the hydraulically bound 
(cemented) layers.  
These are not the only form of distress in a pavement structure  nor are they 
the most common ones. For example permanent deformation in asph alt at 
high temperatures is a distress mode which is not quantified in  the current 
design procedures. Rather, it is considered in the asphalt mix design 
procedure and, hence, it is assumed, for the purposes of paveme nt design, 
that asphalt mixes are sufficiently stable and that the magnitu de of permanent 
deformation is minimised. 
 
7.2.2 Design Considerations 
The design procedure is based on the structural analysis of a m ulti-layered 
pavement subject to normal road traffic loading. The critical l ocations of the 
stresses/strains within a pavement model and the “idealised” lo ading situation 
are shown in Figure 7-1.  
 
 
Figure 7-1: Pavement model 
 
The critical responses that are reflected in the pavement layer s and subgrade 
materials are illustrated in Figure 7-1 and can be summarised a s follows:

Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016  Bituminous layer – manifested by the horizontal tensile strain  a t  
bottom of layer (location 1), 
 Unbound granular – not considered in model, 
 Hydraulically bound layer – horizontal tensile stress at botto m of layer 
(location 2), 
 Subgrade and improved subgrade material – vertical compressive  
strain at the top of the layer (location 3).

using the plate bearing tests and the values of resilient modul us are tabulated 
below. The subgrade criterion should be applicable to traffic c lass of T5-4 to 
T5-0 with a maximum cumulative standard axle load of 1 million.

It is widely accepted that the compressive strain in the surfac e of the 
subgrade is the criterion that governs the total cover required  in the case of a 
flexible pavement. If the compressive strain is excessive, perm anent 
deformation will occur in the subgrade and this will cause defo rmation at the 
pavement surface. 
 
The relationship between the maximum permissible compressive st rain and 
the cumulative number of standard axles is usually given by an empirical

large degree on the stiffness of the underlying material. The c oncept of 
modular ratio limitations in successive unbound layers should b e applied in 
routine design procedures as follows:

For natural material, and graded crushed stone of subbase quali ty the 
maximum moduli should be restricted to 300 MPa. Similarly, the maximum 
modulus in natural gravel (base quality) should be set at 300 M Pa and for 
high quality graded crushed stone (base quality) to 500 MPa. An  assessment 
of the stress level in the unbound granular material is recomme nded in the 
stiffness designation process. 
 
The following presumptive stiff ness values for natural material s will be 
adopted to prepare the pavement catalogue with a Poisson’s rati o value of 
0.35: 
 
 Natural subbase material: 200 MPa 
 Graded Crushed Stone for subbase : 300 MPa 
 
 Natural gravel base: 300 MPa 
 Graded crushed stone base: 400 MPa 
 
7.2.5  Characterization of Hydra ulically Modified and Hydraulic ally Bound 
Materials 
The fundamental mode of failure of hydraulically bound layers i s in tension. 
Every such material has a tensile strength and, if that is exce eded, then the 
material will fail. A hydraulic ally bound layer is therefore in  danger from the 
moment it sets as a solid. 
 In the mechanistic design procedure the following type of mater ials should be 
distinguished: 
 
Strong Hydraulically Bound Layers 
Although it is accepted that even the strongest hydraulically b ound layer will 
crack due to temperature changes, such cracks are almost always  transverse 
in direction. They represent points of weakness but so long as longitudinal 
wheel-path cracking is avoided, the layer still forms an excell ent support to 
overlying materials and gives excellent protection to an underl ying subgrade. 
That all changes as soon as a longitudinal wheel-path crack for ms. The usual 
way to design against such longitudinal cracking is to carry ou t a multi-layer 
linear analysis and to compute the tensile stress at the base o f the layer 
under a design load application. This can be divided by the ten sile strength 
expected from the material to give a stress ratio, and a number  of load

Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 applications to failure determined using a fatigue characterist ic. For heavily 
trafficked highways this ratio should not exceed 40%. The stiffness used for 
the hydraulically bound layer should be the value for fully int act material 
and not the effective modulus of the layer. In other words they are 
designed not to crack longitudinally and a pavement incorporati ng such a 
layer has exhausted “phase 1” of its life as soon as longitudin al cracks occur. 
However, the transversally thermally induced cracks are real en ough and the 
computed stress must therefore be increased by a factor to take  account of 
the increased layer bending which occurs near a crack due to a loss of 
continuity. For cracks with excellent interlock a value of 1.2 could be used and 
for wide cracks values as high as 1.5. 
 
Weak Hydraulically Bound Layers The description “weak” here means that the strength is not inte nded to be 
sufficient to prevent cracking under traffic loading. Actual co mpressive 
strengths will be lower than 10MPa on highways, but stronger ma terial could 
still fail under the classification “weak” where load levels ar e much higher. 
Since cracking is expected, no design against cracking is possi ble. The only 
task is to assign a realistic long-term stiffness modulus to th e layer so 
that overlying layers can be designed correctly.  
 
Shrinkage cracking in materials with a cementitious additive is  inevitable. 
Cracks that reflect to the pavement surface allow the entry of water, which 
frequently accelerates distress through weakening of pavement a nd subgrade 
layers, erosion of cemented material and/or pumping of fines fr om below the 
cemented layers. 
The width of shrinkage cracks is minimised by using low plastic ity materials 
and low treatment strengths. The following measures should be a pplied in 
order to minimise cracking: 
 
Use slow setting binders, which promotes slightly less shrinkag e than the 
OPC (Ordinary Portland Cement). These binders are also likely t o require 
less moisture for compaction which also reduces shrinkage. 
Minimise the clay content of the material to be cemented by con trolling the 
amount and plasticity of fines 
Place a bituminous curing coat as soon as possible after constr uction to 
inhibit rapid drying out of the cemented layer and delay surfac ing as long as 
possible so that cracking occurs before surface placement. 
Where the final seal is to be placed immediately following curi ng apply a 
geotextile to inhibit potential shrinkage induced cracking of t he surfacing. 
Also, whilst this measure does not serve to minimise shrinkage cracking in 
the cemented layer it ameliorates the influence of shrinkage cr acking on 
overlying layers.  
The Australians propose the use of overlying layers of other ma terials in the 
original structure to minimise the extent and/or size of cracks . For example, 
over a cementitiously stabilised layer, placing a polymer modif ied seal and

combination of granular material and asphalt such that 0.75 x ( thickness of 
granular material cover in mm) + (thickness of asphalt cover in  m m )  ≥  1 7 5  
mm.

The Indirect Tensile Strength of hydraulically bound materials is generally 
approximately 1/8 to 1/10 of the UCS value and could be used to  e s t i m a te 
modulus.  For a given strain level, fatigue resistance decreases with inc reasing strength, 
i.e. stronger/stiffer (brittle) materials may tolerate fewer re petitions of load at a 
given strain level than weaker (more flexible) materials. Howev er, as the 
strains generated in a cemented pavement having a high modulus are 
generally quite low, a longer life is realised compared with a cemented 
pavement having a low modulus.

mechanistic method along with a Poisson’s ratio of 0.25:

7.2.6 Bituminous bound material characterisation 
The distribution of temperature, both on a daily and a seasonal  basis, has an 
important bearing on pavement performance. The effect of temper ature 
changes in asphalt on pavement performance is a complex matter which 
must be taken into account at the design stage. 
 
For example, if traffic loading occurs at night when temperatur es are low, and 
the asphalt is relatively brittle, then a considerable reductio n in the life of a 
thin asphalt surfacing may occur due to the onset of flexural c racking. On the 
other hand, if traffic loading o ccurs during periods of high te mperature, then 
this may lead to increased strains in the lower layers of the p avement if these 
layers are composed of cemented material. If the lower layers a re unbound 
materials, however, then the higher stresses induced in the unb ound 
materials will result in higher moduli values being achieved.

For pavement designs, the temperature of the asphalt can be cha racterised in 
terms of the Weighted Mean Annual Pavement Temperature (WMAPT).  The 
WMAPT takes into account the relationship between asphalt tempe rature and 
the fatigue life of thick asphalt pavements. It was derived usi ng a method 
published by Shell in 1978. 
 
In routine design the following procedure should be adopted in estimating the 
WMAPT from air temperatures at each site:  
 obtain from the Meteorological Department the monthly average daily 
maximum air temperature and the annual monthly daily minimum ai r 
temperature  
 calculate the monthly average air temperatures by averaging th e 
maximum and minimum air temperatures 
 using equation 6.1 and the monthly average air temperature, 
calculate the temperature Weight ing Factors (WF) for each month  
 for each site, average the 12 weighting factors obtained in st ep 3 
 using average WF from step 4 and equation 6.2, estimate the 
weighted mean annual pavement temperature (WMAAT) for each site  
 using the WMAAT and equation 6.3, estimate the WMAPT for each 
site. 
 
Shell Weighting Factors (based on Chart W of shell pavement des ign manual) 
– Equation 6.1 :

Effect of asphalt layer's contribution to overall pavement stif fness 
 
Consider, firstly, the extreme situations: the negligible contr ibution to overall 
pavement stiffness provided by thin, very flexible, asphalt sur facings on a 
thick concrete base, and the 100% contribution provided by a th ick asphalt 
pavement on a weak foundation. In the former case, when the pav ement is 
loaded, the tensile strain in the asphalt is dominated by the s tiffness of the 
pavement sub-structure supporting the asphalt and is largely in dependent of 
asphalt modulus. In the latter case, when the asphalt is loaded , the tensile 
stress at the bottom of the asphalt is independent of asphalt m odulus, while 
the strain is inversely proportional to the modulus. Hence, if the asphalt layer 
is the major contributor to the overall pavement stiffness, the  asphalt layer is 
considered to operate in a ‘controlled stress’ mode, i.e. the l evel of tensile 
stress at the bottom of the asphalt layer is controlled by the dominance of the 
asphalt's contribution and is (relatively) independent of the s tiffness of the 
layer. 
Because the fatigue life of the layer depends on the levels of tensile strain 
produced in the layer by the traffic loads, and because these s train levels 
decrease as the asphalt modulus increases, for the ‘controlled stress’ 
situation the fatigue life is increased by increasing the aspha lt modulus.

7-13 
Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 Consider, now, how the situati on changes when progressing from the former 
case towards the latter. As the contribution by the asphalt lay er to the overall 
pavement stiffness increases, the bottom of the asphalt layer w ill start to 
experience tensile stress and strain. If the asphalt's contribu tion to overall 
pavement stiffness is still relatively small, the level of stra in in the asphalt will 
be (relatively) independent of the asphalt modulus. In this sit uation (in 
practice, thin asphalt surfacings), the asphalt is considered t o be operating in 
a ‘controlled strain’ mode because the tensile strain in the as phalt is 
‘controlled’ by the dominance of the stiffness of the pavement sub-structure 
supporting the asphalt and, hence, is (relatively) independent of the asphalt 
modulus. 
Owing to the fact that a stiff asphalt layer can withstand less  applications of a 
given strain than a soft asphalt layer, in the ‘controlled stra in’ situation fatigue 
life is increased by decreasing the asphalt modulus. It should be noted that, in the above discussion, the asphalt i s considered to 
be of one modulus for the entire depth of asphalt. For the ‘con trolled stress’ 
situation, where the asphalt is providing the bulk of the pavem ent stiffness, 
fatigue life can be further improved by using a lower modulus a sphalt with a 
higher bitumen content in the bottom portion of the asphalt whe re it is subject 
to tensile strains. In such a manner, the versatile properties of asphalt are 
best utilised.

ROAD DESIGN MANUAL         PART 3 – Materials and Pavement Design for New Roads  
 
 
9-2 
Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 9 SHOULDERS, PAVEMENT DRAINAGE & CROSS SECTIONS 
 
9.1 Shoulders 
It is that part of the road width between the outer edge of the  traffic lane and the inside 
edge of the ditch, gutter, kerb, or slope, for rural roads or p art of the road between 
the outer left/first lane and the edge support kerb, for urban roads, designed for 
accommodation of stopped vehicles for emergency use. 
 
Ideally, the road shoulders should be an extension of the carri ageway. Thus, there 
would be no question of structural adequacy, that they can supp ort the sides of the 
carriageway and, provided they were sealed, would prevent rainw ater seeping into 
the edge of the carriageway and prevent it from weakening.  However, in reality, because of economical reasons, the shoulde r s  a r e  o f t e n  
constructed of different materials to the carriage way and are not always sealed 
which then leads to their differential erosion and premature we akening of the 
carriageway. 
 
9.1.1 Functions of the shoulders 
The purposes of the shoulders are as follows: 
i. Improve road safety by providing better visibility and conve nient hard 
standing surface for emergency vehicle stopping or passage 
ii. provide lateral support to the pavement layers, especially if granular 
materials are used for the base thus allowing confinement and i ncreased 
stress level, 
iii. Facilitate removal of surface water from the road and inte rnal drainage of 
pervious base by using selected granular material 
iv. Provide space to escape potential accidents or reduce their  severity. 
v. To increase the effective width of the traffic lane to enhan ce driving 
comfort and minimize risk of accidents due to vehicle straying from the 
traffic lane 
vi. Facilitate passage of non-motorized traffic (pedestrians an d cyclists) in 
rural areas to enhance road safety 
vii. To provide for and hence shift the zone of seasonal moistu re variation 
from the carriage way to the shoulder thus reducing occurrence of 
deformation failures due to reduced pavement or subgrade bearin g 
capacity resulting from increased moisture levels during wet se asons. 
9.1.2 Selection of Pavement Materials for the Shoulder 
For shoulders to function effectively, they must be durable to support occasional 
vehicle loads, be impervious to surface water, be properly shap ed so as to shed 
water completely and be erosion resistant in all kinds of weath er without failure. 
Paved or stabilized shoulders are required to minimise the risk  of such structural 
ROAD DESIGN MANUAL         PART 3 – Materials and Pavement Design for New Roads  
 
 
9-3 
Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 failure. Paved shoulders of the same strength and standard as t he pavement layers 
under the carriage way should be considered for road standards DR1, DR2 and DU1, 
DU2. These standards are explained below and details on their c haracteristics are 
provided in the Geometric Design Manual, Part 1 a): 
 
 Standard DR1/DU1: Provides the highest geometric design standa rd 
for rural or urban areas. Roads of this standard usually servic e long 
trips with high speed of travelling, comfort and safety. This s tandard 
always includes divided carriageways with full access control, 
 Standard DR2/DU2: Also provides high geometric standards suita ble 
for long to intermediate trip lengths with high to medium trave lling 
speeds. The road is usually designed with partial access contro l.  
 
For the other type of road standards the following approach is recommended: 
 If the base consists of cohesive material, such as plastic gra vel, or 
hydraulically bound layers then the shoulders might be left uns urfaced, 
 If the base consists of cohesion less material such as graded crushed 
stone or non-plastic gravel, the shoulders must be sealed. A do uble 
surface dressing may be required. 
 For gravel roads, it is recommended that the shoulders be cons tructed 
with the same materials as the wearing course. 
 
The width of shoulders should be as prescribed in the Geometric  Design Manual, 
Part 1 a).  
9.2 Pavement Drainage  
Permeability of the pavement surface contributes to a series of  surface distresses 
generally associated with stripping of the binder from the aggr egate which then leads 
to fretting and ravelling of the surface. Ultimately, this woul d cause loss of asphalt 
homogeneity and loss of bond between the pavement layers leadin g to delamination 
which will have a negative impact on the structural adequacy an d wellbeing of the 
pavement. 
 
Minimizing the amount of water able to percolate in the pavemen t layers is 
paramount and requires protection measures at the sides and und erneath the 
pavement notwithstanding that the surface should be maintained in a sealed and 
impermeable condition. 
 
When evaluating the various aspects of a pavement drainage syst em, one needs to 
consider the surrounding land for complex ground water movement s. 
 
ROAD DESIGN MANUAL         PART 3 – Materials and Pavement Design for New Roads  
 
 
9-4 
Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 9.2.1 Drainage of Carriageway and Shoulders 
Rain falling on the road surface and shoulders must be conveyed  rapidly to the side 
ditches. For this purpose, road and shoulder surfaces are given  a crossfall, the value 
of which depends on the nature or the surface. The following cr ossfalls are 
recommended: 
 
 Bituminous and concrete road surfacings :  2.5% 
 Earth and gravel road surfaces                  : 3 to 4% 
 Gravel shoulders                                       :  4% 
 Primed or cement treat ed shoulders         :  6%

9.2.2 Internal drainage of  pervious pavement layers 
When permeable base materials are used, particular attention mu st be given 
to the drainage of the base layer. The “trench” type of cross-s ection, in which 
the pavement layers are confined between continuous impermeable  
shoulders, must be avoided.  
 The main cases to consider are as follows: 
9.2.2.1 Pervious Base and Impervious subbase 
It would be ideal to extend base and subbase across the shoulde rs. However, 
except for narrow shoulders, thi s solution is extremely costly.   
In most cases, the drainage or the base shall be ensured by a c ontinuous 
drainage layer, placed under the shoulder at the level of the u nderside of the 
base. This drainage layer shall consist of proper filter materi al (graded 
crushed stone, sand, etc.) and should be at least 75 mm thick.  
9.2.2.2 Pervious Base and Pervious subbase 
Although costly, the only effective solution is to extend the s ubbase across the 
shoulders to the d rainage ditches.  
ROAD DESIGN MANUAL         PART 3 – Materials and Pavement Design for New Roads  
 
 
9-5 
Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 9.2.2.3 Impervious Base. 
Provided that the shoulders are sufficiently impervious and the  joint between 
base and shoulder is properly sealed, internal drainage should not be 
necessary.

9.3 Pavement cross-sections 
Based on the above considerations, four alternative cross-secti ons are presented in 
Figure 9-2.  It is to be noted that, unless the base course mat erial is extended fully 
across the shoulders, some extra width is nevertheless provided  for the base. This 
provides support to the edge of the pavement, where compaction is difficult to 
achieve. The extra width of the base course should be on the or der of 20 to 30 cm. 
The edge seal covering the extra width of the base and the join t should extend a total 
of 40 to 60 cm. 
 
The fifth cross-section i.e. Type E, uses curbs, as is occasion ally required in urban 
areas. It is to be noted that, since the drainage of the base c ourse is impeded, it is 
essential that internal drainage be provided by a pervious subb ase or a drainage 
layer. 
Side drains should be avoided in areas with expansive soils. If  side drains cannot be 
avoided due to site conditions, they shall be kept at a minimum  distance of 4 - 6 m. 
from the toe of the embankment, dependent on the road functiona l classification. 
Side slopes shall also be flattened to 1:6 or flatter. A more t horough discussion of 
expansive soils is given in Chapter 10. 
 
9.3.1 Edge Restraint  
It is essential that the edges of the roadbase are given suffic ient lateral 
support, so that they can resist the stresses applied by heavy vehicles. This 
problem is particularly serious in the case of cohesion less ma terials, such as 
graded crushed stone. In this respect, it should also be noted that proper 
compaction of the edges is difficult and lower densities freque ntly result. Two 
possible solutions are available as  described below:  
       (i)  Extra width of base 
This is the most common solution and consists of laying a roadb ase wider 
than the bituminous surfacing, so that the base edges are not t rafficked. It 
applies to all types of base. The extra width shall be 200 300 mm on each 
side. It shall  be primed and sealed  together with the inner e dge of shoulder 
(total width of edge seal: 400- 600 mm).        (ii)  Kerbs  
Concrete kerbs may be placed instead of this extra width. Howev er, such 
kerbs are expensive and will be justified only in the case of a  graded crushed 
stone base or in urban sections.  
 
ROAD DESIGN MANUAL         PART 3 – Materials and Pavement Design for New Roads  
 
 
9-6 
Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 Moreover, they create a discontinuity along the edges, ·with po ssible cracking 
and subsequent water ingress. Kerbs along roadbase are therefor e 
recommended only where a pervious subbase is laid.

Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 10 PROBLEMATIC SOILS 
 
10.1 Introduction 
This chapter describes particular subgrade soils which can have  
serious implications in roads if not addressed correctly. They are 
described as ‘Low strength soils’, ‘Expansive soils’, ‘Saline s oils’ 
and ‘Organic’ soils. 
10.2 Expansive Clays 
Comparatively large areas of Kenya are covered with expansive 
clays, commonly known as “black cotton soils”. These clays have  
caused persistent difficulties in road construction. The guidel ines 
presented below should be followed during the design and 
construction of roads on such expansive soils.  
 
10.2.1 Geotechnical characteristics of Expansive Clays 
Some clay soils undergo slow volume changes that occur 
independently of loading and are attributable to swelling or 
shrinkage. These volume changes can give rise to ground 
movements which can lead to cracking and breakup of the road th ey 
support. Furthermore, these volume changes may produce lateral displacements (“creep”) of the expansive clay, if the side slop es are 
not gentle enough. 
 
The principal cause of expansive clays is the presence of swell ing 
clay minerals such as montmorill onite. Differences in the perio d and 
amount of precipitation and evapotranspiration are the principa l 
factors influencing the swell-shrink response of a clay soil be neath 
the road pavement. Trees with high water demand may dry out cla y 
causing shrinkage. 
 
The potential for volume change in clay soil is governed by its  initial 
moisture content, initial density or void ratio, its microstruc ture and 
the vertical stress, as well as the type and amount of clay min erals 
present. These clay minerals are responsible primarily for the intrinsic expansiveness whilst the change in moisture content o r 
suction controls the actual amount of volume change, which a so il 
undergoes at a given applied pressure. 
 
There are two modes of swelling in clay known as intercrystalli ne 
and intracrystalline. Interpartic le swelling takes place in any  type of 
clay deposit irrespective of its mineralogical composition, and  t h e  
process is reversible. In relatively dry clays the particles ar e held 
together by relict water under tension from capillary forces. U pon 
ROAD DESIGN MANUAL         PART 3 – Materials and Pavement Design for New Roads  
 
 
10-3 
Ministry of Transport and Infrastructure, Republic of Kenya Apr il 2016 wetting, the capillary force is relaxed and the clay expands. I n other 
words, intercrystalline swelling takes place when the uptake of  
moisture is restricted to the external crystal surfaces and the  v o id  
spaces between the crystals. Intracrystalline swelling, on the other 
hand, is characteristic of the smectite family of clay minerals , and 
montmorillonite in particular. The individual molecular layers,  which 
make up a crystal of montmorrillonite, are weakly bonded so tha t on 
wetting water enters not only between the crystals but also bet ween 
the unit layers which comprise the crystals. Generally kaolinit e has 
the smallest swelling capacity of the clay minerals and nearly all of 
its swelling is of the interparticle type. Illite may swell up to 15% but 
intermixed illite and montmorillonitemay swell some 60 to 100%.  
Swelling in calcium montmorillonite is very much less than in s odium 
variety, it ranging from about 50 to 100%. Swelling in sodium montmorillonite can amount to 2000% of the original volume, the  
clay then having formed a gel. 
Because expansive clays normally possess extremely low 
permeabilities, moisture movement is slow and significant perio d of 
time may be involved in the swelling-shrinkage process. 
Accordingly, moderately expansive clays with a smaller potentia l to 
swell but with higher permeabilities than clays having a greate r swell 
potential may swell more during a single wet season than more expansive clays. Cemented and undisturbed expansive clay soils often have a high  
resistance to deformation and may be able to absorb significant  
amounts of swelling pressure. Therefore, remoulded expansive 
clays tend to swell more than their undisturbed counterparts. 
 
When the moisture content increase, expansion occurs and the 
bearing strength of the black cotton soil decreases dramaticall y. The 
CBR may be reduced to less than 2% if the soil becomes completely saturated.   
When dry, black cotton soils present a sand like texture (polyh edral 
segments formed by the agglomeration of clay, silt and sometime s 
sand particles). In this state, they are prone to erosion to a much 
greater extent than that normally anticipated from their plasti city and 
clay content.  
 
10.2.2 Identification and classification 
The investigations to identify and to classify expansive soils 
according to their expansiveness are presented below: 
 
 Routine Investigations are those carried out during surveys of  project

 In-Depth Studies include specialized laboratory testing and is  u s e d  
when extended investigations show occurrence of expansive soils , 
and the required countermeasures have high economic 
consequences. 
 
Routine investigations  are those analyses carried out during 
normal centerline soils surveys and site observations, includin g 
simple geological and geomorphological assessments, field 
reconnaissance, routine indicator tests of Atterberg limits and  
grading.
